Convergence and Diversity in the Control Hierarchy
Alexandra Butoi, Ryan Cotterell, David Chiang, [1ex] ETH Zürich

Abstract
Weir 1992 has defined a hierarchy of language classes whose second member ( ℒ 2 \mathcal{L}_{2} ) is generated by tree-adjoining grammars (TAG), linear indexed grammars (LIG), combinatory categorial grammars, and head grammars. The hierarchy is obtained using the mechanism of control , and ℒ 2 \mathcal{L}_{2} is obtained using a context-free grammar (CFG) whose derivations are controlled by another CFG. We adapt Weir’s definition of a controllable CFG to give a definition of controllable pushdown automata (PDAs). This yields three new characterizations of ℒ 2 \mathcal{L}_{2} as the class of languages generated by PDAs controlling PDAs, PDAs controlling CFGs, and CFGs controlling PDAs. We show that these four formalisms are not only weakly equivalent but equivalent in a stricter sense that we call d-weak equivalence . Furthermore, using an even stricter notion of equivalence called d-strong equivalence , we make precise the intuition that a CFG controlling a CFG is a TAG, a PDA controlling a PDA is an embedded PDA, and a PDA controlling a CFG is a LIG. The fourth member of this family, a CFG controlling a PDA, does not correspond to any formalism we know of, so we invent one and call it a Pushdown Adjoining Automaton.



1
Introduction

Weir 1992
defined a hierarchy of formal languages whose first level (
\mathcal{L}_{1}
) is the class of context-free languages, and whose second level (
\mathcal{L}_{2}
) is the class generated by several existing formalisms: tree-adjoining grammars
Joshi et al. 1975
, linear indexed grammars
Gazdar 1988
, combinatory categorial grammars
Steedman 1987
and head grammars
Pollard 1984
, which were proven weakly equivalent in a classic paper
(
Joshi et al. 1991
;
Vijay-Shanker and Weir 1994
)
, and embedded pushdown automaton (EPDA) as well
(
Vijay-Shanker 1987
)
.

Weir’s hierarchy is obtained using the mechanism of
control
, and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
is obtained by using a context-free grammar (CFG) to control another modified CFG, called a
labeled distinguished CFG
or LD-CFG. Here, we define a controllable pushdown automaton (PDA), called a
labeled distinguished PDA
(LD-PDA), and show that PDAs can also be used as controllers. By combining a controller CFG or PDA with a controlled LD-CFG or LD-PDA, we obtain a total of four formalisms that characterize
\mathcal{L}_{2}
.

\text{CFG}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}\text{CFG}
\text{CFG}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}\text{PDA}
\text{PDA}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}\text{CFG}
\text{PDA}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}\text{PDA}
spinal TAG
LIG
EPDA
spinal PAA

Figure 1:
Overview of results. “
X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}Y
” means “
X
controlling
Y
.” Thin lines indicate
d-weak equivalence
, while thick lines indicate
d-strong equivalence
.

We show that these four formalisms are not only weakly equivalent but equivalent in a stricter sense that we call
d-weak equivalence
. Furthermore, using an even stricter notion of equivalence called
d-strong equivalence
, we make precise the intuition that a CFG controlling a CFG is essentially TAG, a PDA controlling a PDA is an EPDA, and a PDA controlling a CFG is a LIG. The fourth member of this family, CFGs controlling PDAs, does not correspond to any existing automaton we know of, so we invent one and call it a Pushdown Adjoining Automaton (PAA).

The main contributions of this paper are:

•

Adapting Weir’s LD-CFG to pushdown automata (LD-PDA).

•

Two new definitions of equivalence between formalisms,
d-weak equivalence
and
d-strong equivalence
.

•

Four
d-weakly equivalent
formalisms (one old, three new) obtained by controlling an LD-CFG/PDA using a controller CFG/PDA, three of which are
d-strongly equivalent
to TAG, LIG and EPDA, respectively.

•

A new formalism, PAA,
d-strongly equivalent
to the fourth formalism.

2
Preliminaries

Let
[n]
denote the set of integers
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[i\mathord{:}j]}
denote the set of integers
\{i,\ldots,j\}
.

For any sets
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}}}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\in\mathcal{X}\}
and
\mathcal{X}[\mathcal{Y}]=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}]\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\in\mathcal{X},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\in\mathcal{Y}\}
.

2.1
Context-free grammars

We assume familiarity with CFGs; see
section

A.1
for definitions. The following normal form will be convenient later.

Definition 1
.

A CFG is in
normal form
if its productions have one of the forms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}_{k}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}
.

A CFG derivation is sometimes thought of as a sequence of rewriting steps, and sometimes as a tree. The distinction is important in this paper, and we always refer to the former as
derivations
and the latter as
derivation trees
.
See
section

A.1
,
29
for a definition and
fig.

2
a for an example.

2.2
Pushdown automata

Definition 2
.

A
pushdown automaton
(PDA) is a tuple
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta},(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{S}}),(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f},\varepsilon))
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}
are finite sets of
states
,
input symbols
and
stack symbols
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
is a finite set of
transitions
, and
(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{S}})
and
(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f},\varepsilon)
are called the
initial
and
final configurations
, where
q_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota}},q_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{S}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}
. The transitions are of the form
p,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\xrightarrow{a}q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
, where
p,q\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}^{*}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}\cup\{\varepsilon\}
. We say that a transition
scans

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}
and is
scanning
iff
|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}|>0
.

This definition is similar to that of
Hopcroft et al. 2006
but has only a single final configuration.
Stacks are represented as strings over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}
, from top to bottom. Thus, in the stack
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}_{n}
, the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}_{1}
is at the top of the stack, while
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}_{n}
is at the bottom.

See
section

A.2
for further definitions. We again define a normal form that will be convenient later.

Definition 3
.

A PDA is in
normal form
if its transitions have one of the forms
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
or
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}\varepsilon
.

Unlike with CFGs, there is essentially no difference between treating PDA derivations as sequences of transitions or as (unary-branching) trees. See
section

A.2
,
34
for a definition and
fig.

2
b for an example.

3
Equivalence of Formalisms

We think of grammars and automata generically as “formal systems” that have derivation trees yielding strings.
By equipping formal systems with derivation trees, we can define a notion of equivalence that is stricter than weak equivalence, and another notion that is more precise than strong equivalence.

Definition 4
.

A
formal system

F
over alphabet
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
is a set
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}D}
of
derivation trees
together with a
yield function

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}D}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}^{*}}

that defines a language
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}})=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Y}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}D}\}
.

Definition 5
.

Two formal systems
F
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}^{\prime}
are
d-weakly equivalent
if there is a bijection
\phi
between the derivation trees of
F
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}^{\prime}
such that for any derivation tree
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}
of
F
that yields the string
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{s}}
, the derivation tree
\phi({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d})
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}^{\prime}
also yields
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{s}}
.

Example 1
.

Consider two CFGs
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{\textrm{fin}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{\textrm{inf}}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{\textrm{fin}}

\displaystyle=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}\}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{\textrm{inf}}

\displaystyle=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}\}.

These grammars are weakly equivalent, but not
d-weakly equivalent
, because
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{\textrm{fin}}
has just one derivation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}
, but
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{\textrm{inf}}
has infinitely many, so there is no bijection between their derivation sets.

Although there is a standard method to remove unary productions
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}
, on weighted grammars this method requires the semiring of weights to be complete; it will not work with general semirings.
D-weak equivalence
captures this distinction.

Example 2
.

Consider a CFG
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{aa}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}\}
and PDA
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{aa}}=\{p,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}p,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}A};p,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}}p,\varepsilon\}
. Both
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{aa}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{aa}}
derive just one string,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{aa}}
, each with a single derivation. Thus,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{aa}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{aa}}
are
d-weakly equivalent
.

Strong equivalence is traditionally understood to mean that two formal systems generate the same sets of structural descriptions, but this notion cannot be made precise without defining what structural descriptions are. For our purposes, it will suffice to use derivation trees, thought of as unlabeled, unordered trees.

Definition 6
.

Two formal systems
F
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}^{\prime}
are
d-strongly equivalent
if there is a bijection
\phi
between the derivation trees of
F
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}^{\prime}
such that for any derivation tree
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}
of
F
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}
and
\phi({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d})
are isomorphic as unlabeled, unordered trees, and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}
and
\phi({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d})
yield the same string.

Example 3
.

fig.

2
shows the derivation trees of the string
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{a}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{a}}
in the CFG
G
and the PDA
P
defined in
example

2
.
G
and
P
are
not

d-strongly equivalent
, because there is no isomorphism between their derivation trees: the derivation tree in
G
is branching while the derivation tree in
P
is not.

(a)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}

(b)

(p,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}})
(p,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}A})
(p,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}})
(p,\varepsilon)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}
\varepsilon

Figure 2
:
Example derivation trees for
example

2
. (a) For CFG
G
, the nodes are nonterminals/terminals. (b) For PDA
P
, the nodes are configurations.

In general, CFG and PDA are
d-weakly equivalent
(
appendix

D
,
proposition

7
), but not
d-strongly equivalent
.

4
Control

After a number of formalisms were proven to be equivalent characterizations of a language class
\mathcal{L}_{2}
that is slightly more powerful than context-free languages (CFLs),
Weir 1992
introduced the control hierarchy to characterize an infinite sequence of language classes, starting with CFLs and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
. The essential idea is that
\mathcal{L}_{2}
languages can be recognized by a CFG in which the productions do not apply freely, but are
controlled
by another CFG.

4.1
LD-CFGs

To make a CFG controllable,
Weir 1992
augments it slightly, as follows.

Definition 7
.

A
labeled distinguished context-free grammar
(LD-CFG) is a tuple
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{S}})
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}
are finite sets of
nonterminal symbols
,
terminal symbols
and
labels
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
is a finite set of
productions
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{S}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}
is the
start symbol
.
The productions have one of the forms

\ell:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}\rightarrow\alpha{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{B}}}}}\beta
or

\ell:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}\rightarrow\alpha
,
where
\ell\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{B}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}}}}
, and
\alpha,\beta\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma})^{*}}
.

The labels enable a
controller
, which is a formal system over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}
, to decide what productions the LD-CFG (the
controllee
) can use. Because the controller generates/accepts strings over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}
, whereas an LD-CFG derivation tree has a branching structure, the distinguishing marks (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B}}}
) determine the paths of the derivation tree that are controlled.

Definition 8
.

A
sentential form
of an LD-CFG is a pair
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W})
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma})[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}^{*}}])^{*}}
is a sequence of terminal and nonterminal symbols, each augmented with a string of labels, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}^{*}}
is a set of
control words
.

Definition 9
.

If there is a production
\ell:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{B}}}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
,
then we write

({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W})\xRightarrow{\ell}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}[\varepsilon]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{B}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w}\ell]\,\boldsymbol{\beta_{2}}[\varepsilon]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W})
.

If there is a production
\ell\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\beta}
, where
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}^{*}}
,
then we write

({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W})\xRightarrow{\ell}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}[\varepsilon]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w}\ell\})
.

Definition 10
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}^{*}}
.
If

({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{S}}[\varepsilon],\emptyset)\xRightarrow{\ell_{1}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}_{1})\xRightarrow{\ell_{2}}\cdots\Rightarrow{\ell_{n}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}_{n})

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}^{*}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}_{n}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}
, we say that
G

derives

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}
under
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}
.

Example 4
.

Below is an LD-CFG
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{1}
and the derivation tree for the string
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{aabbccdd}}
.

\begin{aligned} {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{1}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}&\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}}}}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{2}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}&\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{B}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}}}}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{3}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}&\rightarrow\varepsilon\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{4}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}}&\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{5}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{B}}&\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{6}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}&\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{c}}\\

The control words used in this derivation are
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}_{4}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}_{5}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}_{6}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}_{7}}\}
.

Definition 11
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}^{*}}
. The
language
of
G
under
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}
is the set of strings
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}})=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{s}}\mid\text{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}$ derives ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{s}}$ under ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}$}\}
.

Definition 12
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}_{2}
be a formal system defining strings over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{1}
an LD-CFG.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}_{2}
controlling
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{1}
form a single formal system, which we call
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}_{2}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{1}
and which defines the language

\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}_{2}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{1})=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}_{2}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{1})
.

Example 5
.

Below is an example CFG
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{2}
that can be used as a controller for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}_{1}
above (
example

4
).

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\varepsilon

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{i}

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{i}\quad(i\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[1\mathord{:}3]})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{i}\quad(i\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[4\mathord{:}7]})

It generates the language
\{\ell_{1}^{n}\ell_{2}^{n}\ell_{3}\mid n\geq 0\}\cup\{\ell_{i}\mid i\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[4\mathord{:}7]}\}
, which makes the LD-CFG generate the language
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{c}}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{d}}^{n}\mid n\geq 0\}
.

The controller could also be a PDA, like
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}_{2}
:

\displaystyle q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}

\displaystyle q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}

\displaystyle q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}q,\varepsilon

\displaystyle q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{i}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{i}}q,\varepsilon\ (i\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[1\mathord{:}3]})

\displaystyle q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{i}}q,\varepsilon\ (i\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[4\mathord{:}7]})

4.2
LD-PDAs

Weir 1992
’s definition of LD-CFG can be adapted to pushdown automata.

Definition 13
.

A
labeled distinguished PDA
(LD-PDA) is a tuple
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta},(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{S}}),(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}))
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}
are finite sets of
states
,
input symbols
and
stack symbols
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
is a finite set of
transitions
, and
(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{S}})
and
(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}^{*}}])^{*}}
are the
initial
and
final configurations
.
The transitions are of the form

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}\colon q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}r,\boldsymbol{\alpha}
,
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}\colon q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}r,\boldsymbol{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{B}}}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}
,
q,r\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{B}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}}}
, and
\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}^{*}}
.

Example 6
.

Below is an example LD-PDA,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}_{1}
, where
q
is both initial and final:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{1}\colon q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}}}}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{2}\colon q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{B}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}}}}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{3}\colon q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}q,\varepsilon

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{4}\colon q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}}q,\varepsilon

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{5}\colon q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{B}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}}q,\varepsilon

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{6}\colon q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{c}}}q,\varepsilon

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{\ell}}_{7}\colon q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{d}}}q,\varepsilon

Definition 14
.

A
configuration
of an LD-PDA
P
is a tuple
(q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W})
, where
q
is the current state,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}^{*}}])^{*}}
is a sequence of stack symbols, each augmented with a string of labels, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}^{*}}
is a set of control words.

Definition 15
.

If there is a transition
\ell\colon q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}r,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{B}}}}}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
,
we write
(q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W})\xRightarrow{\ell}(r,\boldsymbol{\beta}[\varepsilon]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{B}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w}\ell]\,\boldsymbol{\beta}[\varepsilon]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W})
.

If there is a transition
\ell\colon q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}r,\boldsymbol{\beta}
,
we write
(q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w}\ell\})\xRightarrow{\ell}(r,\boldsymbol{\beta}[\varepsilon]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w}\ell\})
.

Definition 16
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}^{*}}
.
If
(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{S}},\emptyset)\xRightarrow{\ell_{1}}(q_{1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}_{1})\xRightarrow{\ell_{2}}\cdots\xRightarrow{\ell_{n-1}}(q_{n-1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n-1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}_{n-1})\xRightarrow{\ell_{n}}(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f},\varepsilon,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}_{n})
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}_{n}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}
, and for
i=1,\ldots,n
, transition
\ell_{i}
scans
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}_{i}
, then we say that
P

accepts
string
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}_{2}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}_{n-1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}_{n}
under
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}
.

Definition 17
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}^{*}}
. The
language
accepted by
P
under
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}
is the set of strings
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}})=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{s}}\mid\text{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}$ accepts ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{s}}$ under ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}W}$}\}
.

Definition 18
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}_{2}
be a formal system defining strings over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}_{1}
an LD-PDA.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}_{2}
controlling
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}_{1}
form a single formal system, which we call
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}_{2}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}_{1}
and which defines the language
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}_{2}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}_{1})=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{F}}_{2}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}_{1})
.

4.3
Four two-level formalisms

Considering both CFGs and PDAs as both controllers and controllees yields four two-level formalisms, one of which is Weir’s original two-level grammar, and the other three of which are new.

Proposition 1
.

CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG, CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA, PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA and PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG are
d-weakly equivalent
.

Proof.

See
appendix

D
.
∎

From now on, we will use different fonts for the symbols in the controllee and the controller. In a controllee LD-CFG/LD-PDA, we use
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}},\ldots
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{b}},\ldots
for the nonterminal/stack symbols and terminal/input symbols. In a controller CFG/PDA, we use
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit B},\ldots
for the nonterminal/stack symbols and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit a},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit b},\ldots
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell_{1}},\ldots
for the terminal/input symbols.

5
Derivation Trees of Two-Level Formalisms

In order to show that our four two-level formalisms are d-strongly equivalent to other
\mathcal{L}_{2}
formalisms, we need to establish what derivation trees look like in these formalisms.
Weir actually gives two definitions of derivation in a two-level grammar. The first is the one we have reproduced above, where a partial control word is written inside the square brackets. The second runs a leftmost derivation of the controller CFG inside the square brackets.
In this section, we follow this second approach, except that we use derivation trees, not leftmost derivations.
We give a separate definition for each two-level formalism.
Ideally, these definitions would have followed “for free” from the definitions of derivation trees of (LD-)CFGs and (LD-)PDAs, but we save this level of generality for future work.

CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}_{2}}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}_{3}}]
\epsilon
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}]
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}_{2}}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}_{2}}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{c}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{B}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}_{2}}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}]
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}_{2}}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}_{2}}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{c}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{B}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}_{2}}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}]

\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}_{1}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}_{1}}]

PDA

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{B}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{B}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{S}}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{L}}_{3}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{C}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{D}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{S}}_{2}]
\epsilon
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{d}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{d}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{c}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{c}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}

Figure 3:
Derivation trees of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{aabbccdd}}
under the four two-level formalisms considered in this paper. For pushdown automata, states are omitted.

Figure

3
shows the derivation of string
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{aabbccdd}}
under the four two-level formalisms considered in this paper. The rest of this section discusses each formalism in turn. We assume all controllers and controllees are in
normal form
; see
appendix

C
for the general case.

PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA

For PDA, derivations and derivation trees are the same thing, so derivation trees of PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA follow Weir’s definition straightforwardly.
There are three cases to consider.

(a) If a derivation node is labeled
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[s,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
,
and
s,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}}q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f},\varepsilon
is a controller transition,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}
is a controllee transition
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}r,\varepsilon
,
then the node can have child
q,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[s,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}
{r,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}

(b) If a derivation node is labeled
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[s,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\boldsymbol{\beta}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
,
and
s,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}}t,\varepsilon
is a controller transition,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}
is a controllee transition
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}r,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{d-1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{d+1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}_{k}
,
then
q,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[s,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\boldsymbol{\beta}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}
{r,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}_{1}[q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}_{d-1}[q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}[t,\boldsymbol{\beta}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}_{d+1}[q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}_{k}[q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}

(c) If a derivation node is labeled
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[s,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\boldsymbol{\beta}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
,
and
s,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}t,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is a controller transition,
then
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[s,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\boldsymbol{\beta}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[t,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\boldsymbol{\beta}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}

PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG

This formalism is not much more difficult than CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG.

(a) If a derivation node is labeled
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]
,
and
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}}q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f},\varepsilon
is a controller transition,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}
is a controllee production
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}

(b) If a derivation node is labeled
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\boldsymbol{\beta}]
,
and
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}}r,\varepsilon
is a controller transition,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}
is a controllee production
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{d-1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{d+1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{k}}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\boldsymbol{\beta}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}_{k}[q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]
\mathclap{\cdots}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}_{d+1}[q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}[r,\boldsymbol{\beta}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}_{d-1}[q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]
\mathclap{\cdots}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}_{1}[q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]

(c) If a derivation node is labeled
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\boldsymbol{\beta}]
,
and
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}r,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is a controller transition,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\boldsymbol{\beta}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[r,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\boldsymbol{\beta}]

CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG

When the controller is a CFG and therefore has branching derivation trees, the controllee’s derivation becomes fragmented. To ensure that the fragments form one correct derivation, we need to add some extra information to the derivation nodes: each node is labeled
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}
is a controller nonterminal, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}
are controllee nonterminals or
\bot
. We write
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{\bot}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]
as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]
.

Moreover, the yield of a subderivation is in general a discontinuous string. We write
*
to indicate a hole in a string (meant to resemble foot nodes in TAG), and we write
u(v)
to plug
v
into the first hole in
u
; that is, if
u=u_{1}*u_{2}
where
u_{1}
does not have a hole, then
u(v)=u_{1}vu_{2}
.

(a) If a derivation node is labeled
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}
is a controller production,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}
is a controllee production
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}

The yield is
a
.

(b) If a derivation node is labeled
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}
is a controller production,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}
is a controllee production
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{d_{i-1}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{d+1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{k}}
,
then
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{k}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]
\mathclap{\cdots}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{d+1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{d-1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]
\mathclap{\cdots}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]

If each child node
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{i}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]
has yield
w_{i}
, then the parent has yield
w_{1}\cdots w_{d-1}*w_{d+1}\cdots w_{k}
.

(c) If a derivation node is labeled
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit B_{1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit B_{k}}
is a controller production,
then
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{k-1}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{k}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit B_{k}}]
\cdots
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{0}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{1}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit B_{1}}]

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{0}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{k}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{k-1}}
can be any controllee nonterminals.
If the children have yields
w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{k}
, then the parent has yield
w_{1}(w_{2}(\cdots(w_{k})))
.

CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA

The derivation trees of CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA are the most complicated. To cut down on complexity, we make use of the fact that every PDA that accepts by empty stack is equivalent to one with only one state
(
Hopcroft et al. 2006
)
, and indeed the equivalence is d-strong (
appendix

A
,
proposition

6
). Therefore, we assume that the controllee has just one state,
q
.

Derivation nodes are labeled
(q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}})
, where
q
is a state and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
is a string of symbols of the form
\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]
(as above, for CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG) or
*
.

(a) If a derivation node is labeled
q,*^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
,
where
*^{?}
denotes either the presence or absence of a
*
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}
is a controller production,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}
is a controllee transition
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}q,\varepsilon
,
then
{q,*^{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}
{q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}

If the yield of the child is
w
, then the yield of the parent is
*^{?}aw
.

(b) If a derivation node is labeled
q,\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}
is a controller production,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit\ell}
is a controllee transition
q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{d-1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\breve{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{d+1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{k}}
, then
{q,\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}
{q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{d-1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]*{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{d+1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{k}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S_{2}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}}

The yield of the parent is just the yield of the child.

(c) If a derivation node is labeled
q,\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit B}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit B}_{k}
is a controller production,
then
q,\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
q,\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{k-1}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{k}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit B_{k}}]
\cdots
q,\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{0}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{1}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit B_{1}}]\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{0}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{k}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Z}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}_{k-1}}
can be any controllee nonterminals.
If the children have yields
w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{k}
, then the parent has yield
w_{1}(w_{2}(\cdots(w_{k})))
.

The reader may be surprised that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
is placed on the leftmost child.
It would be equally sensible to place it on any of the children, but the leftmost child makes the yield function simplest.

6
Correspondences with
\mathcal{L}_{2}
Formalisms

We can now show how PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA, PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG, and CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG are d-strongly equivalent to EPDA, LIG, and TAG, respectively, and to introduce PAA, which is d-strongly equivalent to CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA.

6.1
Embedded pushdown automata

Definition 19
.

An
embedded pushdown automaton
(EPDA) is a tuple
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta},(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S}]),(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f},\varepsilon))
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}
are finite sets of
states
,
input symbols
and
stack symbols
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
is a finite set of
transitions
, and
(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S}])
and
(q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f},\varepsilon)
are called the
initial
and
final configurations
, where
q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\iota},q_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}
. The transitions are of the form
p,[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}q,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}^{\prime}
or
p,[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}q,\varepsilon
, where
p,q\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}^{*}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}([{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S}])^{*}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}\cup\{\varepsilon\}
.

An EPDA maintains a stack of stacks. Each transition can pop and push from the top stack, as well as pop the top stack and/or push other stacks above and below it.

Proposition 2
.

An EPDA is
d-strongly equivalent
to a PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA.

Proof.

See
appendix

E
.
∎

6.2
Linear indexed grammars

Definition 20
.

A
linear indexed grammar
(LIG) is a tuple
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{S}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S})
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}
are sets of
nonterminals
,
terminals
and
stack symbols
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
is a finite set of
productions
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{S}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}
is the
start symbol
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}
is the
initial stack
. The production rules are of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]\rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\mathinner{\cdot\cdot}]{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}_{2}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Y}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}^{*}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{\Upsilon}}_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{N}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S}]\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma})^{*}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}\cup\{\varepsilon\}
.

Each nonterminal symbol is augmented with a stack (in square brackets). A production with lhs
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}
rewrites a nonterminal
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}]}
into its rhs,
while popping
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}
and pushing zero or more stack symbols.
1
1

1

Most LIG definitions
Vijay-Shanker 1987
;
Joshi et al. 1991
;
Kanazawa 2014
allow only productions that pop/push at most one symbol from/to a nonterminal’s stack.
Exactly one of the rhs nonterminals inherits the stack, while the others get new stacks.

Proposition 3
.

A LIG is
d-strongly equivalent
to a PDA
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG.

Proof.

See
appendix

E
.
∎

Figure 4:
Example TAG and derivation tree for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{aabbccdd}}
. To reduce clutter, symbols have been renamed. Symbol
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{X}}

Definition 21
.

A
tree-adjoining grammar
(TAG) is a tuple
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{G}}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{C},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}})
where
\mathcal{V}
,
\mathcal{C}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
are finite sets of
variable
,
constant
,
2
2

2

The terms
variable
and
constant
come from
Lang 1994
and are equivalent to nonterminals with obligatory and null adjunction constraints, respectively.

and
terminal symbols
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S}\in\mathcal{V}
is the
start symbol
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
is a set of
productions
. The productions are of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}\rightarrow\beta
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}\in\mathcal{V}
and
\beta\in\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{C}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}\cup\{\varepsilon\})
. In
\beta
, interior nodes must be in
\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{C}
. There is one leaf in
\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{C}
that is designated the
foot node
and marked with
∗
. The path from the root to the foot node is called the
spine
. Other leaves must be in
\mathcal{V}
(in which case they are called
substitution nodes
and marked with
↓
) or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}\cup\{\varepsilon\}
.

This definition is close to
non-strict TAG

(
Lang 1994
;
Rogers 2003
)
. Unlike the usual definition, its rules have left-hand sides and do not require the root and foot node to have the same label.
We also do not allow substitution, as it can be simulated by adjunction. A TAG derivation starts with

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S}

and proceeds by repeatedly choosing a variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}
and rewriting it using a production
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}\rightarrow\beta
, such that the children of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}
become the children of
\beta
’s foot node. See
section

B.3
for additional definitions.

Definition 22
.

A TAG production
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}\rightarrow\beta
, where
\beta
has a foot node, is called
spinal
3
3

3

This term comes from
Fujiyoshi and Kasai 2000
, who use it for context-free tree grammars.
if every node of is either on the spine or the child of a node on the spine.
A TAG production
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}\rightarrow\alpha
, where
\alpha
does not have a foot node, is called spinal if there is a leaf
\eta
such that every node is either on the path from the root to
\eta
or the child of such a node.
A TAG is called spinal if all its productions are spinal.

TAG derivation trees were defined by
Vijay-Shanker 1987
.
We show an example TAG and derivation tree in
fig.

4
.

Proposition 4
.

A spinal TAG is
d-strongly equivalent
to a CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
CFG.

See
appendix

E
; we give a brief sketch here, using the same three cases as in
section

5
.

Figure 5:
Example PAA with run and derivation tree for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{aabbccdd}}
. To reduce clutter, symbols have been renamed. Symbol
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{X}}
is constant; other uppercase symbols are variable.

A pushdown adjoining automaton (PAA) maintains a stack of symbols. The stack symbols are either
variable
symbols, which can be rewritten, or
constant
symbols, which cannot. Each stack symbol is augmented with another stack, written below it:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{B}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{C}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{D}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{E}}

Here, the top-level stack is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{A}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{B}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{C}}
, in which
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{B}}
is augmented with stack
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{D}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{E}}
. The other symbols have empty stacks, which are not shown.

A PAA transition has a lhs which is a variable symbol
and a rhs which is a stack (as described above).
The rhs may have one symbol occurrence called the
foot
; if so, both the lhs and the foot are written as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}
.

At each step, the PAA operates on the topmost stack that has a variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}
on top. If it has a transition
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}
, then it scans
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}
, pops
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}
and pushes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}
had a stack attached, then the stack becomes attached to the foot of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}
.
Finally, any constant symbol that has an empty stack is deleted.

Example 7
.

The PAA for
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{c}}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{d}}^{n}\}
is shown in
fig.

5
, along with the run of this PAA on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{aabbccdd}}
.

The nested stacks of a PAA, described informally above, are treated formally as trees with a special constant symbol
\top
at the root. However, we continue to draw them as shown above, without
\top
.

Definition 23
.

A
pushdown adjoining automaton
(PAA) is a tuple
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textbf{P}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{C},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S})
where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma}
,
\mathcal{V}
, and
\mathcal{C}
are finite sets of

input symbols
,
variable stack symbols
, and
constant stack symbols
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
is a set of
transitions
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit S}\in\mathcal{V}
is the
initial stack
.
The transitions are of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}\in\mathcal{V}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}\in\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{C})
.
The root of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}
is
\top
, and at most one leaf in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}
is designated the
foot
and written as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}
.

Definition 24
.

The path from the root to the foot of a PAA production’s rhs is called its
spine
.
We say that a PAA production is
spinal
if every rhs node is either on the spine or a child of a node on the spine.
A PAA is
spinal
if all of its productions are spinal.

Definition 25
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}\in\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{C})
without constant leaf nodes, the
top variable
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}
is defined as follows: If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}
, then the top variable is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}=\hbox to26.9pt{\vbox to20.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.53471pt\lower-14.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

Definition 26
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}
has top variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}
, and there is a transition
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tau}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}\rho)
,
then we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tau}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}^{\prime}

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}^{\prime}
can be obtained from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}
by replacing its top variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}
with
\rho
(sans
\top
), making the children of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}
the children of the foot of
\rho
, and deleting any constant leaf nodes.

In a PAA derivation tree (see
fig.

5
for an example), every node is a stack, written without its embedded stacks.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
is such a node and
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit X}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{a}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi})
is a transition, then the stacks of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xi}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
are its children.

Proposition 5
.

A spinal PAA is
d-strongly equivalent
to a CFG
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangleright}}
PDA.

Proof.

See
appendix

E
; we give a brief sketch here, using the same three cases as in
section

5
.

Case (a) corresponds to the rule

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{X}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsfit A}]\xrightarrow{a}\varepsilon

Case (b) corresponds to the rule
